Feature · page 18

BILL LUCAS "... a bold spirit in a loyal breast"

CLIVE SHIPPEN

It was in 1948 in the AAA Championships at White City that Bill first came to my notice. My 14 yrs. old classmates and I had been taken there by our enthusiastic P.E. teacher ("killer" Clark of SLH and later Hercules AC) to witness our first major meeting. We knew no-one of course, so when the 3 miles field swept round the first bend we decided to pick our winners rather like viewing horses in the pre-race paddock. I opted for a tall, stylish runner who looked a thoroughbred compared with many of the others. He was entered in the programme as W.E. Lucas, Belgrave H.; right next to G.F. Lucas of the same club. My ignorance soon became apparent when Sydney Wooderson became the first Briton to break 14 minutes for the distance, closely followed by the flying Dutchman Willie Slykhuis. Fortunately betting was strictly forbidden, as Bill was well out of the medals.

Not until the following year's visit did W.E. Lucas come to mind again. There he was once again in the programme together with G.F. Lucas (Bels) and L.W. Herbert (Bels). Once again I was out of luck; Lucas was in the medals alright (3rd) but it was the wrong Lucas!

In 1948 I hovered. This year the Championships were also the Olympic trials. Willie Slykhuis must be favourite surely and Alec Olney was already making a name for himself. I decided to back Bill for 3rd (one of my class-mates had decided to run a book this year). This time my loyalty paid off: Bill finished a brilliant 3rd and I made 5 shillings! As a result of his run Bill was selected for Great Britain in the 1948 Olympic Games.

Four years later, coincidentally, I found myself a member of Belgrave Harriers. An early visit to Belgrave Hall found me enquiring who the fellow was with the clip-board: he looked familiar. On being told it was Bill Lucas I can still remember bursting out with "Not the W.E. Lucas". I was looked at rather oddly as Belgrave Harriers were blessed with several classy runners at that time and Bill would have been ribbed no end if Tom Carter, Footer and Co. had heard my utterance. Fortunately they didn't hear about my five bob win either.

For the next 40 years or so Bill's imposing figure strode the stage of athletics fixtures and committees throughout England and I often wonder how many others have said "Not the W.E. Lucas!" There would have been many different reasons for saying this of course, for Bill has always been noted for speaking his mind; whether it be to a recalcitrant team member or the fully arraigned South of England Committee. He has been respected for it. But above all is his deep loyalty to Belgrave.

He could so easily have become an international track referee, but he preferred to support Belgrave fixtures. He fought for Belgrave's interests in every forum. And can anyone remember a Belgrave Dinner without his presence? The Club has benefitted immensely from his membership these last 61 years.

And should he ever reach those Pearly Gates and announce himself in his toastmaster style, no doubt a voice will be heard to boom "Not the W.E. Lucas!"

CLIVE SHIPPEN

Left: Bill Lucas attempts to work his way though the field during the Boxing Day 2 miles handicap 1948. Tooting Common was covered in frost and the cinder track was bone hard.

Left: Ted Pallant, President, congratulates Past President Bill Lucas on his achievements.

Right:Suzy and Leo Coy, Denny Manning, Snowy Brooks, Don Turner, Alan Black (obscured), Alan Mead, Charlie Henn and Marianne Black listen to the speeches.

Left: Enjoying the occasion. Pat Mead, Tony Binda, Marianne Black, Gerry North, Alan Black, the late Laurie O'Hara, George Piddington, Charlie Henn, Ted Pallant, Chris Steer, Charlie Manning.

Sadly this was the last time that we saw Laurie O'Hara. He died the following day while out running.

Feature · page 19

You Had Better Believe It!

Eric Hall

Memory plays funny tricks – or so it is said. Can we believe what we read? The facts are twisted on occasions no doubt but when they are recorded in official reports it should be possible to rely on them, or at the very least there should be some acceptable explanation for the discrepancies.

For many years one of my hobbies has been historical research, and, as some may recall, an earlier interest was race walking. This last year gave me the chance to combine the two and given that during a visit to an Internet site a year or so ago we found that our own Stan Vickers was said to be German I was ready for anything.

The Olympic Museum in Lausanne, Switzerland, has a marvellous collection of exhibits depicting all aspects of the history of the Games and, in addition, there is a fine library and film archive containing records of every Games of the modern era. The library is open to all during normal opening hours and some video footage is available on screens either free of charge or for a small fee. In addition, by prior notice, it is possible to view other films from the archives and, if your pocket is deep enough, one may also purchase copies for personal use.

During two days spent in the library and studios Mavis and I were able to relive our visit to Rome in 1960; see the Melbourne footage of my own and Stan's events, plus we had time to look through Games reports produced by the host cities and other National Olympic Committees. It was here that my memory proved fallible – or was it?

Starting with an event which means much to a Belgravian but which took place in the year I was born we checked the Tommy Green story in two publications. The British Olympic Association report states that only seven of the fifteen starters finished and that Tommy and Dalinsch of Latvia were together only a mile from home. "The Latvian complained of cramp in his side, and the Italian (Frigerio) began to catch him. Dalinsch, responding gamely, fought off the challenge but, meanwhile, Green was forging ahead to finish more than seven minutes in front of the Latvian, who had a two minute lead of Frigerio, who collapsed on crossing the line".

The German Olympic report on the same race has much the same story except that Tommy got away in the last three kilometres but it shows ten finishers! The other interesting point about the German report is that there are two photographs purporting to be of the same event – in one Tommy is wearing his British vest and number 98 (on his shorts which is not allowed today!) and in the other he is wearing a half sleeved vest, no number, but, possibly, a Belgrave badge! This latter picture also appears in the British report and is likely to have been an agency photograph I would think.

Moving on to 1960, and the 20 kilometre walk, there may be some members of the Belgrave party who made the trip to Rome who would like to know that according to a photograph in the German report Stan Vickers is shown as winning the 50 kilometres although the title is Don Thompson! The American report of 20 kms. notes that "Vickers sinks exhausted to the turf. Two over-zealous Italian medical orderlies lift him up and before he knows what is happening he is whisked off in a Red Cross van to hospital where some British officials discover him hours later in perfect health. The Italian orderlies had failed to understand Vickers' protestations – in English – that there was nothing on earth the matter with him".

Garbled to say the least but it must be official – the Americans say so! For those of you who have been brought up on a different account of events I prefer the British report which, correctly, states that it was Matthews who finished up in hospital after retiring at halfway. It is true that Stan's Italian was not too good but then what could you expect of someone who was described as being a German anyway!

Don't always believe what you read – the truth is stranger than fiction but fiction is said to be more interesting – sometimes!

Eric Hall

Stan VickersMavisTommy GreenDalinschFrigerioDon ThompsonMatthewsEric Hall

Report · page 19

Bill Lucas's 80th birthday

A fine gathering of Belgravians turned out at Belgrave Hall on April 19th to celebrate Bill's 80th birthday and over 60 years of continuous service to the Club (with no signs of his letting up just yet!).

A succession of articulate speakers took us back to the days of Bill's running, his selection for and appearance at the 1948 Olympics in the 5,000m, then his years of announcing at the White City. Verbal battles at Southern Counties A.A.A. were recalled, anecdotes of his exploits as an official: "... get those toes behind the line Ovett or I'm disqualifying you and your whole team!" – and two terms as President of our Club. Thanks to the wonders of modern technology Clive Shippen was able to telephone from Australia at exactly the right point in the proceedings with his words being broadcast to all present.

With Bill having spent a considerable period as Belgrave's and Surrey's Cross Country Team Manager it was fitting that many of his team were able to attend or send messages: Gerry North, Alan Black, Laurie O'Hara were there – even Chris Steer had managed to fly in from North America. How wonderful to see George Piddington back at the Hall as well.

Gordon Biscoe and Charlie Manning masterminded the event with Derek Manning arranging the catering. Well done chaps – and well done Bill.

Below: Snowy Brooks, Ted Pallant and Sheila, Bill Lucas and Sheena enjoy the festivities at Belgrave Hall on April 19th.

Above: Ted Pallant and Denis Brickwood recall old days and enjoy the 'spread'.

Feature · page 20

Born to run

Richard Grant

The Tarahumara Indians of northern Mexico do no training, wear home-made sandals, smoke cheap cigarettes and drink corn beer - and still thrash the gringo opposition in 100-mile ultra-marathon events. By Richard Grant. Photos by Richard D. Fisher, R.Glenn, Leadville Picture Company. With acknowledgement to Esquire magazine in which this article first appeared

Standing there, silent and perfectly still in the pre-dawn darkness, the seven Tarahumaras look like men from another time, marooned in the late twentieth century. They are dressed in loincloths and loose white tunics, their bodies small and light, copper-brown faces calm and impassive. As usual, I have no idea what they are thinking. I have spent the last two weeks with these men, journeying from their homeland in the Sierra Madre mountains of northern Mexico here to Leadville, Colorado, sharing accommodation, campfires, meals and beers, and I have come to know them not at all. A vast and seemingly unbridgeable cultural gulf lies between us. The Tarahumaras are what the anthropologists call a "non-verbal people", who value silence over speech, and I live by the word.

Today they will run a 100-mile race over mountain trails, at altitudes of between 10,000 and 12,640 feet. I find this inconceivable, but they do not seem to regard it as any big deal. For the last few days they have sat around smoking cheap, harsh, filterless Mexican cigarettes, and making their running sandals from old tyres scavenged at the Leadville City Dump. Yesterday, I asked them in Spanish how they felt about the race, and after a long silence, one of them nodded and said, "We will win." I wrote it down solemnly, the best quote of the day.

Hundreds of professional and amateur ultramarathon runners, mostly white Americans in polypropylene clothing and hundred-dollar shoes, are milling around the start line, backslapping and gladhanding, smiling at the television cameras, yelling last-minute instructions to their support crews. Next to the stoic, silent Indians, they look like caricatures of themselves: noisy, brash, goofy, spoilt, histrionic, anxiety-ridden.

And the words just pour out of them. I ask them what it takes to be an ultrarunner and fill my notebook with talk of total dedication, short-twitch muscle fibre, positive mental attitude, the importance of sound nutrition and a scientific training programme incorporating track, trail, strength and speed work. When I tell the white runners about the crude sandals and the pile of cigarette butts in the Tarahumaras' quarters, some of them smile and sigh and shake their heads. Others get sour and hostile. The Indians are getting "special treatment". They are destroying the sense of "community" among American ultra-runners. They have no place running in American races because they don't speak the language or share the culture.

The Tarahumara Indians may well be the best long-distance runners on the planet but they follow no training schedule. In fact, they avoid running altogether, unless it is after wild game, or to win bets during inter-village races. For a Tarahumara, sound nutrition means avoiding starvation between corn harvests, and, consequently, they eat deer, skunks, coatimundis, squirrels, rats, lizards, snakes, eels, grasshoppers and worms; in short, anything that walks, flies, swims or crawls. Sixty per cent of them have tuberculosis, they all seem to smoke, and their drinking habits are almost as legendary as their running activities. Once or twice a week, from childhood to the grave, Tarahumaras get falling-down drunk on a home-brewed corn beer known as tesguino.

Their powers of physical endurance have long puzzled our scientists, and several physiological studies have been carried out. The results are not conclusive but suggest that their running ability is not a genetic talent, but a state of physical conditioning resulting from the arduousness of daily life in their homeland. Tarahumaras routinely walk 40 or 50 miles in a day with heavy loads, through mountain and canyon country too steep and rugged for horses. They live a hard, dirt-poor, primitive existence considered to be the most traditional Indian lifestyle left in North America.

In 1992, a photographer and wilderness guide from Tucson, Arizona named Richard Fisher brought a contingent of Tarahumara runners to race in the Leadville 100 for the first time. They dominated the early part of the race, then all mysteriously dropped out at the 40-mile mark. Last year, Fisher tried again, and to his astonishment Tarahumaras finished first, second and fifth, running in their home-made rubber sandals or huaraches, and stopping off for beers at the 87-mile mark. The winner, Victoriano Churro, was 55 years old and ran the hundred miles of trail in 20 hours and three minutes.

Manuel Luna, the fifth-place finisher, is the only member of the 1993 team to return this year. Recruiting Tarahumaras to race is an extremely haphazard process and Fisher knows virtually nothing about the other six runners under his charge. They come recommended by their village chieftains, and showed no signs of fatigue after a 25-mile trial run at 11,000 feet, but otherwise they are an entirely unknown quantity, They could be undiscovered world-class athletes or just along for the ride.

None of the sports journallists here in Leadville give the Indians a chance of winning the race. Everyone's money is on Ann Trason, a 33-year-old Californian who holds six women's ultramarathon world records, and has won five races outright. "When she is at her best," according to a recent Sports Illustrated profile, "ultra-marathon's gender gap becomes the distance between her and the men eating her dust." she is the epitome of the modern scientific athlete, under sponsorship from Nike, trained by professional coaches, enslaved to the stopwatch.

"Go Ann! Go Ann! Go Ann!" At precisely 4am the 1994 Leadville Trail 100 gets underway. The great pack of runners, 349 in total, surges forward into the darkness and illuminates it with their torch beams. Ann Trason is up near the front, a small wiry woman with lean, muscular, beautiful legs. The Tarahumaras are running as a group on the outside of the pack, white tunics flowing out behind them, fluid and graceful as the movement of clouds.

The year is 1892, and a genial Norwegian naturalist and explorer named Carl Lumholtz is leading an expedition into the mountains of north-western Mexico, a remote, mysterious, bandit-infested range known as the Sierra Madre. He is chasing a rumour about a lost tribe of cliff-dwelling Indians, possibly descended from the people who abandoned the great cliff citadels of the American south-west. The country is wild and forbidding, a place of steep-sided gorges, rock pinnacles and sudden storms. It is January and the trails are obliterated by snow.

Lumholtz finds no cliff-dwellers, but does come across a large tribe of cave- and hut-dwelling Indians, who call themselves the Raramuri, or "Foot-runners". The local Mexicans hire them to run wild horses into corrals and know them by the mispronunciation "Tarahumara". "Their endurance is truly phenomenal," Lumholtz writes. "They will run down a deer, following it for days through snow and rain, until the animal is cornered and easily shot with arrows, or until it is overtaken, utterly jaded and its hoofs dropping off."

He witnesses races between different villages, in which runners cover 100 miles, 130 miles, 170 miles, kicking a carved wooden ball the whole way. The villagers gamble heavily on the outcome of the races, and employ shamans to cast spells and hexes on the opposition. Some of the runners take peyote before they race, believing that the plant's spirit will safeguard them against witchcraft. "A race is never won by natural means," notes Lumholtz drily. "The losers always say they have been bewitched by the others."

He is charmed and fascinated by the Tarahumaras, and in 1894 he returns to the Sierra alone and lives with them for eighteen months. He learns their language, takes peyote with their shamans, records the ceremonial songs, and attends numerous corn-beer binges, or tesguinadas. At the end of it he concludes, "The Tarahumara in his native condition is many times better off, morally, mentally and economically than his civilised brother - but the white man will not let him alone as long as he has anything worth taking away... It may take a century yet before they will be made servants of the whites and disappear. . . "

A century later, I am riding a rat-infested, first-class train through the Sierra Madre and reading Lumholtz's Unknown Mexico. I put down the book and look out the window. The sky is full of vultures, soaring above rock spires and thousand-foot waterfalls, the mountains covered with a strange forest of cactus and thorn trees. The armed guards that come with a first-class train ticket stand up and take positions between the carriages. Bandits are still a menace in these mountains. They hold up the train on a regular basis, stripping passengers of their money and jewellery, then riding away on horseback. Two months ago they shot the conductor, while tourists looked on and took photographs.

At Divisadero, I see the first Tarahumaras. The women are weaving baskets from wild grasses and cooking up greasy goat-meat tacos, babies nursing at their breasts. I buy a stuffed squirrel and a hand-carved violin and stand on the rim of the barrancas, a convoluted system of eroded river canyons, deeper and wider than the Grand Canyon of Arizona. This wild, primeval, remote country is the last refuge of the traditional Tarahumaras. The high forested plateau has been invaded by logging companies, bringing roads Mexican settlers, environmental catastrophe and cultural upheaval The best arable land has been seized at gunpoint by the Mexican drug cartels in order to cultivate marijuana and opium poppies. But down in the barrancas, there are still Tarahumaras living as Lumholtz left them, wearing the loincloth, hunting with the bow and arrow, running down their game, living in caves and grass-thatched huts. It is from these traditional communities that Richard Fisher tries to recruit runners for Leadville.

"Go Ann! Go Ann! Go Ann!" At the 13.5-mile aid station, Ann Trason comes through in the lead, a few minutes ahead of a Californian runner named Kurt Madden and two Tarahumaras 42-year-old Martimiano Cervantes and 25-year-old Juan Herrera. The Tarahumaras say Martimiano is very strong, and has won many kickball races for his village. He certainly looks like a tough old bastard, and bears an uncanny resemblance to Geronimo. Yesterday morning he came down to breakfast in a pink, floppy-brimmed woman's hat he had found somewhere, looking stern and stoical as ever, then breaking into a broad grin. Juan has emerged as the group's leader and spokesman. He speaks excellent Spanish and has experienced the world beyond the Sierra before, visiting his modernised relatives in the foul slums of Chihuahua City, Cuauhtemoc and Juarez. I have ascertained nothing about his running abilities, but his confidence is unshakeable. I hand him a banana and ask him how he's doing. "I will catch her and win," he says, then runs off into the dawn.

The other five Tarahumaras all come through in the top 20, and none of them have yet broken sweat. They began the race with their splayed, bark-like feet encased in trail shoes. donated by the sponsors of the race, but have since changed into huaraches and are running effortlessly. At this early stage, everything seems to be going smoothly, but Tarahumara racers have a way of confounding people's expectations of them.

There have been several attempts this century to prove the Tarahumaras' running prowess outside its cultural context, and most of them have failed. At the 1928 Olympics, Mexico entered two Tarahumaras in the marathon, and imagined the gold and silver to be a foregone conclusion. But the coaches made the mistake of using words describe the distance of the race, rather than taking the Tarahumaras around the route and showing them. The runners failed to understand that the race was a mere 26 miles, and paced themselves for the much longer races they were accustomed to. They crossed the finish line a few minutes behind the winner and kept on running until the officials stopped them. "Too short, too short," they said.

The Leadville 100, an ultramarathon over rugged mountain trails, would seem ideally suited to their talents, but Fisher has run into countless unforeseen problems. In 1992, they ran at night with their torches pointing upwards, as they do with burning pine brands in the races back home. They considered it rude to take food or water from the aid stations, unless it were offered to them by hand first. He still doesn't fully understand why they all dropped out at 40 miles, because none of them seemed to be fatigued. Last year, he invited runners from rival villages and this, he thinks, was the key to the team's success. The Tarahumaras were racing against each other, and just happened to pass all the gringos along the way. This year he has promised a ton and a half of corn and half a ton of beans to the village whose runners perform the best. This, we think, is their primary motivation for racing, but they have also heard all about Ann Trason, and seem interested in beating her. The rumour in the Tarahumara camp is that she is a sorceress of some kind, and that this explains her phenomenal success in running against men. The rumour circulating around Ann Trason's camp is that the Tarahumaras will drop out, or somehow freak out, if they are passed by a woman during the race.

At 30 miles, she is well inside course-record pace, grim-faced and sweaty, but running with great poise and grace. Juan and Martimiano are sitting right on her heels. Fisher is worried that the pace is too fast for them, that they will burn themselves out but, to me, the Indians look impossibly fresh. Thirty miles and neither of them has a bead of sweat to show for it. They take turns pressing Ann Trason, harrying her, overtaking and dropping back, working her like a deer.

Left to right: Traditional running gear in the Mexican hills; cutting out a tyre to make running sandals; the Leadville 100; Ann Trason, women's ultrarunning champion

The village is called Tatahuiche and consists of a few wooden huts around a sawill. Next to the sawmill is a huge mound of saw-dust, wood shavings and logging slash, and perhaps a hundred Tarahumaras are sitting on it, silent and motionless. The young men are in polyester slacks and Western shirts, but the old men are still wearing loincloths, bright-coloured headbands and baggy, puffed-sleeve shirts. The first shirts the Tarahumaras saw were worn by the Spanish conquistadors and they have stuck with the style ever since. The women are swathed in shawls and head-scarves, and wear three or four skirts at once. When the outside one gets dirty, they move it to the inside. When I approach them they scoop up their children and disappear into what remains of the forest. Long and bitter experience, from the conquistadors to the present day, has led Tarahumara women to regard all white and Mexican men as potential rapists.

The kickball racing has died out in this village, as it always does when the logging roads come, but today there will be an attempt to revive the tradition. The state Tarahumara Co-ordination Agency has invited runners here from all over the Sierra. After the race, Rick Fisher plans to take the best of them to the Leadville race. It seems like a simple, logical plan - but this is Mexico. The men's race was supposed to begin yesterday, and then this morning, and now it is mid-afternoon and the Indians show no sign of stirring. They have been sitting like this for four or five hours, for no reason that Rick and I can fathom. They seem to inhabit some alternative reality, to which we have no point of access.

The Tarahumaras call us, whites and Mexicans alike, chabochis, "the bearded ones", and believe we are all the children of the Devil. We have no respect for the natural world or our fellow human beings and create disharmony wherever we go. We are to be pitied for this rather than scorned because, as children of the Devil, we cannot help the way we are. The Tarahumaras have no conception of our linear, empirical systems of thought, and do not share our devotion to the word, or our enslavement to the clock. Theirs is a world where time moves in seasons, where shamans flit in and out of animal forms and the souls of the dead deliver messages in dreams, where night is the day of the moon.

Fisher is an intelligent, perceptive man who has spent twelve years around Tarahumaras and has read all the ethnographies: "What I have come to realise is that whatever you or I are thinking at a particular moment, they are thinking something entirely different. We know a little about their philosophy and religion, but there is something fundamental to the way they perceive reality that we don't understand at all."

Sometime in the mid-afternoon, the Tarahumaras all rise as one, although nothing has been said and no signal been given. The village gobernador walks over to us and says, "Now we race." Rick Fisher has been angling for a long, gruelling men's race, to mimic the conditions in Leadville, but now there is no time and the Tarahumara want to stage a boys' race first. Everyone ambles over to a rocky plateau, the course is laid out by pointing at various streams, cornfields and hillsides, and then the gambling begins. A mound of bets piles up at the feet of the village gobernador: beads, dresses, skirts, and cash money.

The boys' race lasts two hours, and generates considerable excitement. They have not seen a race here for fifteen years, and have forgotten the proper procedures for staging one, but everyone seems to enjoy themselves immensely. Boys, young men and grandfathers all join in the race, running alongside their team, shouting encouragement, pointing out the position of the wooden ball - doing everything in their power to try to win their bets. When the race is over, the winning boys sit down on their own, and receive no cheers, applause, congratulation or even acknowledgement. People collect their winnings and pay the runners no attention whatsoever.

By now, it is late afternoon and the sky is dark with thunderheads. They decide to run a brief exhibition men's race. There are two runners from Norogachic, Martimiamo Cervantes and Juan Herrera, against two from Samachique, Gabriel Bautista and Martin Ramirez. The race is a mere 20 miles or so, no use to Rick Fisher, but a beautiful spectacle nonetheless. The runners wear traditional clothing and seem to flow over the rugged terrain, barely pausing to flick the heavy, wooden ball forward with their feet. By the closing stages, 40 or 50 anxious gamblers have joined in. one of them stops, pulls a skirt off his wife to increase his stake, then carries on, chanting something like "Huite, huite, huite" to spur his team on.

Afterwards, there is a feast in the village. A bloody goat hide hangs dripping from the rafters of the sawmill, the animal's guts floating in a big cauldron of evil-smelling soup. Fisher has a problem. The train bringing runners from Urique has derailed. The truck sent to fetch runners from Choguita has broken down. None of last year's Leadville racers have shown up except Manuel Luna. He needs five more runners to fulfil his contract with the race sponsors in Leadville, and has to leave tomorrow morning. He wants the best runners in the Sierra, but at this stage, anyone will do. It requires long, elaborate negotiations with the village chieftains, and repeated sorties deep into his wallet, but he manages to secure the four runners from the exhibition race. The next day, to my utter amazement, a taxi appears with two more Norogachic runners, and we are headed for Leadville.

The Leadville 100 is not, as its sponsors suggest, the toughest ultramarathon in America, but it is tough enough. After running 40 miles, the runners are confronted by a huge, towering wall of mountain, and the dispiriting knowledge that they must run up it to Hope Pass, at 12,640 feet, and down the other side to the Winfield mid-point. Then they have to turn around and run back over the mountain again. The Winfield aid station looks like a war-zone field hospital. Runners are collapsing in agony, wailing and moaning, puking and pissing blood. Many of the amateurs drop out at this point, unable to face the prospect of Hope Pass again. Ann Trason comes through here looking preternaturally strong and determined, having run over the pass in record-breaking time and opened up a four-minute lead over Juan Herrera, and ten minutes on Martimiano. Kurt Madden, in fourth place, is half an hour off the pace now, and falling further and further behind.

The runners are allowed pacers for the last 50 miles of the race, to run alongside them and offer support. Martimiano, who is suffering from stomach problems, is joined by a good-natured, hippie ex-prize-fighter named Micah True. On the way back up Hope Pass, Martimiano stops, throws his guts up, and says he wants to quit. Micah, who has run the Leadville 100 before, and lost his breakfast on Hope Pass before, tries to talk him through it: "It's hard for every-one. This is the worst of it. I know you can do it. If you can make it to May Queen, you can have a beer." Martimiano is soon back up and running, and Micah swears that it was the prospect of a beer which spurred him on.

Ann Trason, I decide, is bionic. She goes back over Hone Pass at the same relentless speed, opening up a huge gap. At the 60-mile mark, she has 21 minutes on Juan, 46 minutes on Martimiano, and looks unstoppable. Juan is hurting desperately now, his face contorted in pain, his legs beginning to hobble. His pacer, a renowned ultrarunner from

ULTRA CHAMPION Ann Trason holds six world records

She is the epitome of the modern, scientific, sponsored athlete.

San Diego named Jamie Williams, calls for chocolate, coffee, ginseng and pinole, a roasted corn mixture which is a staple of the Tarahumara diet. "And we need Advil," he says. "He'll never make it without painkillers."

We are close to the border now and the lights of Juarez and El Paso stretch as far as the eye can see, drowning out the stars. Juan has been here before, and Manuel passed through last year, but for the rest of them, this is a sight beyond the limits of their imagination. Their eyes are wild, excited, terrified, and they start at the sound of klaxons and squealing brakes. The cars are like ants, they say, and the traffic frightens them because it moves so fast, in patterns they cannot understand. At the border, they get out of the truck in full traditional dress - loincloths, tunics and headbands - and walk into the US Immigration building. For some of them, this is their first experience of air-conditioning, electric strip lights and polished tile floors.

An obese immigration officer interviews them in Spanish about their running, and extols the cheap and easy virtues of American women. He wishes them luck in both regards, waves them through and we are in Texas. We buy a bag full of double cheeseburgers and a case of Budweiser and spend the night camped in a country club out-side El Paso.

For the Tarahumaras, it has been a long bewildering day, but for sheer culture shock, nothing compares to their first experience of a modern American bathroom. In the Sierra, they bathe in the streams and rivers; you see the water and climb in it. Here, there is a mysterious array of knobs, buttons, handles and taps that call forth jets of scalding water, sprays of freezing water, and all manner of strange whooshing sounds.

Light comes from a switch, heat comes in a hot dry blast, and instead of squatting in the forest, one sits atop a strange porcelain throne. The next days are taken up by the long drive north, and more cultural adjustment. Every social interaction among Tarahumaras is conducted with politeness and quiet formality, but here, people are forever charging up to them, seizing their hands in a death grip, and firing off lights into their eyes. The Tarahumaras hate having their picture taken, but say nothing in protest. They know that we cannot help our rude, disrespectful ways. We are, after all, the children of the Devil.

Whether it's chocolate, pinole, ginseng or the Advil I do not know, but Juan is miraculously back from the dead, and gaining ground on Ann Trason. At 76.5 miles, he has cut her lead from 21 minutes to 16, and Jamie is screaming for more painkillers, more pinole, a cup of sugared coffee. As I scramble to get these things ready, one of the race officials says to me, "Relax. Your boy's never gonna catch her. I'll give you 10-1 she wins it."

They take off flying down the trail, Jamie manic and crazed, ranting in Spanish for help from the wind, the elements, the Tarahumara gods. Juan seems far far away, cocooned in some private world of his own. His body must be flooded with endorphins now, the natural opiates which the body releases to fight prolonged pain. Mixed with the adrenaline pumping during a race, the effect is said to be something akin to a speed-ball. This is what ultrarunners get addicted to, the waves of euphoria, the heightening of the senses, the feeling of leaving the body. Running a hundred miles, they say, is like living a life in a day.

Rick Fisher and the rest of the support crew are far behind now, and it looks like I will be crewing Juan and Jamie to the finish line. At the May Queen aid station, 86.5 miles, I line up bottles of pinole, water, painkillers, flashlights, and wait. The television helicopter is hovering over Sugarloaf Mountain, tracking Ann Trason and Juan, and calling through radio reports to the ground crew. She's five minutes ahead. She's two minutes ahead. The lead is 50 seconds. The excitement at May Queen is electric. Peter Severance, from the magazine Running Wild, is freaking out. "He's gone over Sugarloaf like no one has ever gone over Sugarloaf. This is incredible." And I'm thinking, "He's running her down like a deer."

The lead stays at 50 seconds for some time. Juan and Jamie refuse to pass her on the mountain, but stay right behind, pressing and harrying again, trying to upset her rhythm and concentration. It's not until they are down off the mountain that they speed past her, Jamie letting out a wild, blood-curdling war whoop at the moment of glory. By the time they reach May Queen, Juan has a six-minute lead over Ann Trason, and is 45 minutes inside course record time. Martimiano is more than an hour behind in third place, and the rest of the field are at least two and a half hours off the pace. I make the high-speed drive to the next crew access point, ruing that 10-1 bet the whole way.

At 93 miles, Juan is 20 minutes ahead and Jamie is struggling desperately to keep up with him, despite the 50-mile head start. "He comes and goes. He's incredible. I don't know if I can make it," he says, between great gapes of air and gulps of water. I drive on to the finish line. If Juan, in his exhausted, hypersensitive state, thinks he will soon be able to sit quietly on his own, like the runners after a Tarahumara kickball race, he is in for an ugly surprise. The approach to the finish line is an avenue of howling crowds, high-powered lights and television cameras. As he comes into view, a disembodied, grotesquely amplified voice starts screaming out his name into the night, "Let's give it up for JUAN HERRERA! He is 25 years old and running at Leadville for the first time. JUAN HERRERA! He has smashed the course record! Let's have a big Leadville welcome home for JUAN HERRERA!

He crosses the finish line in 17 hours and 30 minutes, 25 minutes inside the course record. He is completely shattered, nauseous and looks like a rabbit caught in the headlights. Jamie and Rick Fisher are weeping and hugging him, and the film crews and photographers are shoving and jostling around him, the cameras coming for him again. He finds refuge in the medical tent, sits down and dry heaves for a while. Ann Trason comes in 36 minutes later, having beaten her own women's course record by more than two hours. Martimiano finishes third and appears only lightly fatigued. He sits down, drinks a couple of beers, and goes to sleep. All seven Tarahumaras finish in the top eleven, and all except Juan drank beers at May Queen.

It is a bad day for the white men. Kurt Madden, their leading representative, finishes in sixth place, more than three hours behind Juan. He shakes his head and smiles and offers them congratulations, but not everybody is so gracious. There is a lot of unseemly sniping and griping after the race, a mixture of racism and sore-loserism. Drinking beer, smoking cigarettes, never training, and finishing first, third, fourth, fifth, seventh, tenth and eleventh, the Tarahumaras have made a mockery of the hallowed principles of the ultrarunning community, and there is a building sentiment that they shouldn't be allowed to compete next year. The corporate sponsors of the Leadville Trail 100, I notice, are conspicuously silent when it comes to the matter of congratulating the winners. This surprises me, but I suppose it shouldn't. The sponsor manufactures hundred-dollar running shoes and sells them to affluent Americans. The race was dominated by dirt-poor Mexican Indians, running in pieces of old tyre strapped to their feet with leather thongs.

FINISHED Juan Herrera after the Leadville 100

Completely shattered, nauseous, he looks like a rabbit caught in the headlights.

South of Thames 'Silver'

Road Running and Cross Country

South of Thames 'Silver'

SOUTH OF THAMES CCA CHAMPIONSHIP FARTHING DOWNS, COULSDON, 8 FEB 1997

16 teams closed in.

South of the Thames Hon. Treasurer and Belgravian Jim Heathfield turned up at Coulsdon wearing his club sweater in anticipation of the Bels being involved in the presentation – and like all good accountants he got it right! We had actually anticipated being in with a chance of taking the team title but had reckoned without the powerful Medway squad who turned out in force to back up their main man Barry Royden. But while the Kent club's white and red vests packed out the early positions, our claret and gold was also well to the fore and we were pleased enough with our team silvers. Moreover, having got a sight of the magnificent shield that is awarded to the SoT Champions our lads started planning immediately for the 1998 race.

We had made great efforts in our attempt to put twelve men on the start line but only managed ten on the day, a pity, because just two teams managed to finish all 12 men – Thames and SLH – and we were leading them right down the line until we ran out of bodies.

Report · page 24

A Surrey League Win

REEBOK SURREY CC LEAGUE DIVISION 1 RACE 4 FARTHING DOWNS, COULSDON, 22 FEB 1997

Back to Farthing Downs and what a treat for the Belgrave faithful that have been waiting patiently for such a day. The Belgrave tide came in at full flood with Gary Staines leading us to our first Division One win since the 1986/87 season. It was in that same season that we last placed as high as 2nd overall – a position that this outstanding victory allowed us to equal as we leapfrogged over the boys from Aldershot. Our progression over the last two seasons since our return to Division One now reads: 1994/95 – 8th, 7th, 7th, 5th; 1995/96 –8th, 7th, 5th, 4th; 1996/97 – 3rd, 5th, 2nd, 1st. Can we keep it going in 1997/8?

It was great to see Darren Mead back in action over the country for the Bels. while 24 year-old Lee Hurst, on his début, took the chance to measure himself up against some of the London Lads. Tony Barden will not be eligible to score for us until next season – what a great asset he will be for future league races – and then it was Jim Estall, leading home the engine room and collecting the Veterans' prize as well. At the back end of our 'ten' it was another vet., Chas. Dickinson, who closed in for us, making up ground strongly at the end and showing that he still has the beating of four or five of our young dogs.

Road Running and Cross Country

Samantha Northey Just Misses the Medals

TSB ENGLISH SCHOOLS CC CHAMPS., NEWARK 1 MAR 1997

The Senior Girls' race was held over 4.5k and Sam Northey, competing in the colours of Dorset Schools, ran to our highest ever position in this event and actually made the inset picture on the front page of AW. Ok so the picture was small but have any other "Belles" made it to the front page yet?

Derbyshire's Karen Fletcher was a clear winner in 14:47 but the silver and bronze medals were won in 14:53 and 54, just 9 seconds ahead of our Sam's 15:03.

Kevin Quinn was our only other representative, placing an unhappy 239th for Surrey in the Senior Boys' 8k race in around 26 mins 45 secs.

NATIONAL CC CHAMPS. STAUNTON PARK, HAVANT 8 MAR 1997

Road Running and Cross Country

NATIONAL CC CHAMPS. STAUNTON PARK, HAVANT 8 MAR 1997

The rules these days allow clubs to enter and run as many men as they like (although one still has to nominate the scorers) but while Midland clubs like Tipton had travelled all the way to the South Coast to turn out dozens of green and white hooped runners, we could only manage three. Our local rivals SLH, Herne Hill and Thames Hare & Hounds all seem to get it on for the 'National' but 9 miles of cross country and Belgrave just don't currently seem to mix.

All credit to Mike Webb though, who had a good run to get his name on the Parker Bowl yet again – although he says he has some way to go yet before he catches up with G.North! Gerry, incidentally was Course Director at Staunton Park. Kevin Quinn was no more than "satisfied" with his effort in the Junior Race but will still be around in the same age group next season when he should be looking at the top 50. As for the Under 15 Boys and females of every age group – we had none!

Report · page 25

PUMA CELL EKIDEN RELAY CRYSTAL PALACE 22 MAR 1997

With Thames Valley's road relay back on the calendar and this Puma sponsored event taking place on the same day as our own road relay, the Belgrave race received very few entries so a late decision was taken to scrap it and take the team over to Crystal Palace instead. The Ekiden, held over the marathon distance, was an interesting race and the big prize money had attracted some top clubs from around the country but when one considered the high standard of runners taking part, there was a surprising lack of atmosphere and it remains to be seen whether the race will be promoted again.

Without any big stars, the Belgrave lads did well in such company, particularly Said Chettati who raced himself into 12-Stage reckoning. As we have never done an Ekiden before our performance must be rated as an inaugural Club Record – although a couple of our top marathon guys might just be able to get inside our 2hrs. 16mins. 35secs. for the marathon distance on their own!

Report · page 25

TVH 5 x 3¼ m ROAD RELAY STOCKLEY PARK, HAYES 29 MAR 1997

Bels were the first ever winners of TVH's relay in 1945 and then shared the spoils with Birchfield until 1950 when the home club got their first look in. One has to go back to 1951 since this event was last won by a Belgrave outfit although our veterans won the over 40 3-stage race in 1973.

A new course in the Stockley Business Park gave a traffic free circuit and as the first leg was completed there was little between the first eleven teams with Windsor leading the procession. Aldershot took a stranglehold on the race on stage two when Kendall turned in his 15:39 and when they followed up with runners who placed 2nd and 3rd on the list of the day's fastest, the result was a foregone conclusion.

We were mixing it well with Shaftesbury, London Irish, etc. but suffered a setback when one of our lads failed to turn up and an unfit Steve Hewes had to be drafted into 'A' team. But then it was Said Chettati churning out another fine run to put us right back into the fight for the prizes.

As Dave Beaver went into the final stage he had Old Gaytonians and London Irish 39 and 25 seconds ahead respectively with Oxford City and Windsor breathing down his neck. The 'City' man, Rashid, immediately leapt ahead but they both overtook the Irish team. Old 'Gayts' were gradually being pulled back and with a mile to go the only certainty was that Aldershot would win. 800 metres and Dave piled on the pressure with Rashid now having to hang on. Turning the final corner, Old 'Gayts' were still clinging on to second place and they just made it by the skin of their teeth while Dave Beaver and Rashid finished as they had set out – three seconds between them and both having run the same time.

19 teams finished Fastest: J.Kendall (Aldershot F&D) 15:39

Report · page 26

SEAA 12-STAGE ROAD RELAY THURROCK, 6 APR 1997

Road Running and Cross Country

SEAA 12-STAGE ROAD RELAY THURROCK, 6 APR 1997

After recent successes even AW writer Martin Duff was tipping Belgrave as 'dark horses' – and that's just how we saw ourselves as late as the Thursday night before the race. Then the rot set in. As long stage runners dropped out, short stagers were moved up to their less preferred distance and the reserves came in. Come Saturday and we had just about gone through our entire allowance of 24 entries. Twelve months earlier we had been delighted with our 8th. Now we were looking for something in the top five and came away disappointed with 10th.

But let us look at it another way. Study the times and the day's rankings for each of our runners and you will soon see how unhappy our long stagers were. Most of them were looking for a short run. Jim Estall was not feeling on top form while injured Nigel Powley was a wrecked shadow of himself. Of our short stage men, Gary Staines, the day's fastest, was most certainly not looking for a head to head over the long stage vs. his marathon rivals with London just 7 days away and we were pleased that he ran at all. Bruce Barton was not at his healthiest and Kevin Quinn was being thrown into the lion's den at 17 years of age; both were not originally scheduled to run yet both held up well. In spite of all this we still finished 10th. Convinced? Your Team Manager is. Sooner or later the luck will be running with us for this race.

Report · page 26

BELGRAVE NONSUCH RELAYS NONSUCH PARK, 10 JUL 1997

What a pleasant bit of mid-summer fun this Thursday evening race was. Organised by Dave Denton and sponsored by our own Bill Laws' company, the Belgrave Nonsuch Relay took in four stages of just over 2 miles of tree-lined, traffic-free road and firm grassland. With the Bels claiming seven of the top eight times (and Donny at equal 13th) one might be led to believe that the race lacked excitement. Far from it!

Dave Beaver was claiming to be only in 'B' team shape – so went in the 'B' team. Ray Foley, as course record holder with 10:45, arrived as the race started and the 'A's already chosen, so he also went into the 'B' quartet. After two stages the 'B's were ahead by 1 second – was it possible that veteran Don could keep close enough to Junior Galley to give Ray a fighting chance? Unfortunately for Don, that's just the sort of pressure that Junior thrives on and it was not to be. Marcello, too, had his pride at stake and although Ray was just two seconds outside his own record, he gained no more than that on the 'A' team.

With a fish and chip supper to follow, the only flaw to the evening's activities was the theft of Don Anderson's IBM van. He had placed his keys in his kit bag near the finish so the thief must have watched him do this and then gone to his bag while Don was running. The moral: give your car keys to the Team Manager.

Report · page 26

London Marathon '97

13 APR 1997

Hopes were high for the Belgrave pairing of Gary Staines and Paul Evans after their Chicago exploits and with Jim Estall and Mike Kazimierski to back them up as a team, we plunged into the hot water of rules & regulations, registered vests, etc. Gordon Biscoe, holidaying in Cornwall during the previous week, spent a great deal of time on the 'phone talking to the SEAA and race referee and once again they allowed our boys to run in a combination of Belgrave vests and Nike Elite vests.

The early pace seemed suicidal. Two Portugese pacemakers took the star-studded field through 5 miles in 24:02 and 10 miles in 48:20 but by Tower Bridge the chances of retaining our team title had gone. Paul Evans was still well in contention but Gary was nowhere to be seen. Eventually a despondant Gary Staines limped past. "Calf muscle's gone," he called out. He'd only kept going because there was no other way to get back to the Tower Hotel but walk and hobble.

The leaders took the half-marathon in 1:03:26 and 20 miles in 1:37:41. There were now only six involved in the chase: Britons Evans and Richard Nerurkar, the Olympic Champion Thugwane of South Africa, Kimaiyo (Kenya), Baldini (Italy) and Pinto (Portugal). With two miles to go the British runners had slipped back and for Paul it was a case of hanging in there on a damage limitation exercise. Pinto also seemed to be in trouble but on the Embankment found a new lease of life to move past Nerurkar, the Kenyan and then get up to the leading pair. Incredibly he still had something left and as Thugwane succumbed to the pressure, he finally outsprinted Baldini as the finish was approached.

We have yet to find out where the Belgrave team placed although they were certainly not in the first three. Kazimierski was about 4½ minutes down on last year's run while Estall was 3½ minutes faster and for some days Don Anderson (the next Belgravian in a club vest) was on tenterhooks in case he might have just sneaked a AAA bronze.

Very many thanks to Lisa Thompson of London Marathon Limited who kindly provided the Belgrave results.

Report · page 27

In the frame and Surrey Champions

Road Running and Cross Country

In the frame and Surrey Champions

HEWITT-JONES WALTON AC 6 x 3m 600y ROAD RELAY inc. SURREY COUNTY CHAMPS., WALTON, 13 SEP 1997

A new season began and we wondered what our fortunes would be over the next six months or so. This race was to give us a good start. Last year we placed third with a team that did not qualify for the Surrey Road Relay Championship held in conjunction. From that team only Roger Alsop remained in our 1997 'A' squad.

Boro' of Hounslow went over half a minute clear on the first stage through Hugh Brasher but the next five teams, headed by Dorking & Mole Valley were all together with Jim Estall nicely positioned at the back of the bunch and handing over to our Junior Captain Kevin Quinn. A 'B' team runner last year, Kev proceeded to reduce his best time for the circuit by 42 seconds, leading the chasing field into the takeover area; gosh! doesn't it do you good to see a young man enjoying his running and with his athletics career all before him!

There were some good guys out on the road for stages three and four. Herne Hill's Jon Solly raced to the day's fastest and sub-16 minute performances also came from Aldershot and Thames Hare & Hounds. In the face of this onslaught Tony Binda and Junior Galley could do little but restrict the damage to a minimum although Tony took 37 seconds off the time he recorded in 1996 as a member of the Belgrave veterans' team.

The field was now a little strung out. Hounslow were out of sight while the Bels were back in 6th but there was tons of excitement to come as the whole lot concertina'd again on stage 5. Sure we were still 6th but Ray Foley was right on the back of the pack, just 2 seconds behind 5th. AFD were looking fairly certain of 2nd but both the first two teams were fielding non-Surrey runners and Thames were believed to have turned out a second-claimer, so that County title was within reach. It was all down to Roger.

Not at his fittest, our Vice-Captain played it cagey and then pounced at the right time. We were in the frame, County Champions, and it had been a bit of real team work. Nice one!

21 teams started, 16 finished.

Fastest: J.Solly (Herne Hill) 15:21

Report · page 27

YACHT HANDICAP 3m 120y, WIMBLEDON, 4 OCT 1997

It was 74 years ago that we ran our first 3 mile handicap around and across Wimbledon Common. Over the years there have been some massive fields but fears for our remaining 'traditional' run were growing as we went into the '90s and the numbers taking part dropped dramatically. The last few races have seen an improvement, however, and the latest running of the the 'Yacht' saw no less than twenty-eight competitors setting out at intervals spread over an amazing 17½ minutes. That spread seems to be getting bigger and bigger but our handicapper know's exactly what he's doing and the whole field finished within 3 minutes.

First back was 17 year-old Wesley Minett in his first Club outing. Wesley, an apprentice chef at Buckingham Palace couldn't have a more knowledgeable tutor when it comes to cooking or running, for his apprentice-master is none other than Lionel Mann, Head Royal Chef and President of Belgrave Harriers.

Judy Oakley placed second and not surprisingly also carried off the prize for fastest woman. Fastest man overall was 43 year-old Jim Estall, looking for a sharpener to celebrate his recent selection for the England Veterans' Cross-Country.

Look out for an old favourite in next year's race. Dave McMillan was just watching this time but within a week or two he was out doing 6 and then 10 mile runs with the Saturday crowd. Just what sort of handicap will 'Boofer' give him when he's already carrying a fair bit of metal to hold him together after his accident? Yep! that'll be worth watching ... but there's a little matter of the 1998 London Marathon to deal with first.

In his first outing in a Belgrave race young Wesley Minett caught the handicapper a beauty.

Photo: Rex Bale

GREAT NORTH RUN NEWCASTLE-NORTH SHIELDS, 14 SEP 1997

What a trio of pbs! Paul Evans's 60:09 from Marrakesh in 1995 has always been suspect due to doubts about the length of the course so his run here where he ended up the first Briton for the fifth consecutive year has got to be given all due credit. Report · page 28

Disappointing start to league campaign

Road Running and Cross Country

Disappointing start to league campaign

REEBOK SURREY CC LEAGUE DIV ONE & WOMEN'S SURREY CC LEAGUE BROCKWELL PARK, 18 OCT 1997

Hopes of improving on last winter's Reebok Surrey League runners-up position took a nose-dive as we slumped to 5th place in the opening fixture at Brockwell Park. Most clubs were short of runners but with the Festival of Running being held in Edinburgh and the Chicago Marathon also being held on the same weekend, we missed our stars more than anyone - except Hounslow!

An administrative error resulted in Herne Hill accepting a home match in the Women's Surrey League Race and Men's Reebok Surrey League on the same day. They decided to go ahead with both sets of races but the afternoon was too crowded and it's unlikely that this will be repeated. The home club suffered a disastrous men's race. The burden of organization plus the fact that their cross-country team manager was getting married and had invited half their team to the celebrations resulted in them placing stone bonkers last.

After a one minute silence in remembrance of Aldershot's Ian Logan the charge was on. Clive Gilby was first Belgravian up the hill and from there on he always headed the Belgrave contingent to finish with a fine 13th place. Coming back from injury problems and running way over his favourite 800 metre distance, he was delighted with his run. Veteran Jim Estall was close behind in 16th and with Roger Alsop 18th the team packed well enough to stay reasonably close on points even though we were unhappy with our lowly team position.

Thames Hare & Hounds took the honours from a surprising Ranelagh Harriers outfit only just promoted from Division Two. Aldershot were 3rd while favourites Borough of Hounslow were only 6th so it's still wide open.

At the time of writing we have still not received results of the Young Athletes races. We do know that the ever-reliable Christian Morris was our only youngster in action and can only hope that he receives a little more support in the forthcoming events.

Our women's teams have slipped since that glorious 1993/94 season when they were unbeatable in local competition. Of the scorers from those days only Helen Maskrey is still turning out in league competition and it looks as if it will be a struggle to avoid relegation this time around. But at least we had runners in each of the age groups and Debbie Hearn in the under 17 race and Alice Ragouzardis-Clark in the under 13s both impressed with their 6th places. Incredibly Alice is actually only eleven and if her interest is maintained she could have a brilliant future.

Report · page 28

Second place at The Priory

REIGATE PRIORY 6 x 2½ miles C.C. RELAY REIGATE, 1 NOV 1997

Belgrave made an early strike on the first lap when Clive Gilby set the day's third fastest time to lead the field home with 13 mins. 13 secs. Clive confessed after his stint to having been, " ... a little bit lazy ... I should have gone earlier." Knowing that he had the legs of Reigate Priory's Alister Moses, he had left it until the last 400m and then took 10 seconds out of him!

Team Captain 'Gerry' Adams found himself pushed into 2nd place by the Priory's Ellis-Smith and then Ray Foley briefly regained the lead on the third stage only for international Jon Solly of Herne Hill Harriers to storm past with an aggressive 12 mins. 59 secs.

From that point on Herne Hill held all the aces with Simon Jones' 13:18, and Ian Munt's 12:56 putting them beyond the reach of the Belgrave outfit which nevertheless continued to draw away from the rest of the field of 39 teams thanks to determined running from Roger Alsop, Junior Galley and Dave Beaver. Good old Dave: a team manager's dream. Not really fit and not really wanting an 'A' run, he nevertheless accepted that he was likely to be a minute faster than the next man even if he ran 'steady' – silver medals were ours as a result.

For the second year in succession Alan Black turned up to watch but ended up making up the numbers. Next year he says he'll not be wearing running kit.

Absolutely brilliant to see Matt Hillier out for us. Now there's a man with talent and if he could just get back into the harness ...

Our young team of Debbie Hearn, Cathy Eastham and Tania Sturton made up the Belles 'A' trio. Most of our local rivals manage to turn out men's and women's teams at the same venues these days and making the effort of getting a team together is half the battle. Success is sure to come sooner or later.

Men 33 teams finished.

Women 21 teams finished.

Tania Sturton (U20W) and Debbie Hearn (U17W) have both been selected to represent Surrey in the Inter-County Championships to be held at Cardiff on March 1st. Also, keep a look out for Rachel Weston who has been awarded an Elite number for the Flora London Marathon.

Report · page 29

South of the Thames "Junior" Win

Road Running and Cross Country

South of the Thames "Junior" Win

SOUTH OF THE THAMES C.C. ASSOCN. "JUNIOR" 5 MILES CRAWLEY, 8 NOV 1997

21 teams closed in.

Brent Shield (8 to score)

9 teams closed in

It's so pleasing when we set out to achieve something, come through the difficulties and then still hit the target. Always pretty sure that we had a good chance of taking this race, we were also hoping to provide the individual winner in Clive Gilby. But Clive knew he had a tough job on – quite apart from the 5 miles of slippery grass and woodlands of Tilgate Forest – he was moving home on the Friday and had a great deal of furniture shifting to do. Not surprisingly, he was pretty stiff and tired come Saturday and regretfully we were going to have to do the job without him.

Now Gerry likes a fast start while Ray Foley prefers to come through as the race progresses, so it was a surprise when the runners poured out of the woods at the end of lap one and it was Ray heading our Captain as they slithered across the muddy playing fields in the bunch chasing Ranelagh's Dave Benton. Among this knot of runners there were also no less than three red and black Brighton singlets. A quick tally of points though and we were happy that things were going our way. We had a trio of runners around the twenty mark from which only two would score while Brighton had to go back to 50 for their fourth man.

A mile or so later and the Team Manager was more inclined to bite his fingers than use them to keep score. Phil Carstairs and Steve Fowler, who had only decided to race a few minutes before the "off" due to sore tendons, had both made up several places but the veteran Ulliot, last scorer for the south coast club, was going like a train and was now just about to go into the 30s.

And so the chase went on to the finish: the two Brighton lads held on to their medal positions but were hotly pursued by two Belgrave vests, the third Brighton man and then the rest of our scorers. Just enough men from other clubs now got to the line before our rivals closed in. We'd done it with a two point margin.

The backing from the rest of the team was great although one had to feel sorry for Mark Anderson who ran well but missed out on the medals. Their reward was to capture the Brent Shield as the team scoring least with all eight men – first time we've ever held it.

151 finished.

... but another 'average' league result ...

REEBOK SURREY CC LEAGUE DIVISION 1 RACE 2 WIMBLEDON, 15 NOV 1997

Now this was disappointing. As a couple of our men went down with 'flu', Herne Hill turned out for our home match en masse. It's just about possible for us to win the league – but unlikely. Very pleasing, however, was the organization of the event. We had plenty of marksmen out on the course, dealt with the obstruction caused by a fallen tree and produced the results in good time. Well done boys.

Report · page 30

The Box Hill-Billies Return!

Road Running and Cross Country

The Box Hill-Billies Return!

SURREY C.C. CHAMPIONSHIPS, FRIMLEY, 13 DEC 1997

Herne Hill were reckoned to be our Senior Men's big rivals as the Surrey Champs. returned to Frimley for what could be the last time. But as it turned out, Boxhill Racers, with their first man only 10th, managed to get all their runners home before any other club and their close packing carried the day. All credit too to South London Harriers, currently leading the second division of the Surrey League team but here only six points away from pulling off a surprise victory.

For the Bels. it was a missed opportunity to put our name on the F. N. Pickett Cup in successive seasons. Sickness hit our team hard with two of our men being unable to turn out at all. But then again, the fact that we were disappointed is a sign of how far we've come – a few years ago we'd have been thrilled to bits with bronze medals. Behind the teams in medal positions came Herne Hill on 149 and Dorking & Mole Valley 156 – any of the clubs in second to fourth positions could have collected the title if they had one more strong runner, it was that close.

The reason that we will probably not return to Frimley is the fact that the sports field on which all the races had previously started and finished, was suddenly made unavailable during the week before the event. The organisers were faced with the task of finding a suitable start point in the woods and reorganising the circuits so that the required distance was covered. A finishing funnel was erected among the closely packed pine trees and each of the races started close by – apart from the senior men whose 200 starters were hemmed into a path on the ridge just 5 metres wide. Needless to say the start was ferocious as those with serious intentions were forced into an all out sprint to secure a forward position.

At the end of the first small lap it was Roger Alsop, floating over the brow of the hill and looking for all the world as if would be quite happy to break away. Kassa Tadesse followed some 10 metres behind with Herne Hill's Jon Solly, South London's Stuart Major and Hercules Wimbledon's Adrian Marriott in close attendance. Then it was Solly taking the initiative with Marriott and Major both dropping away while Roger Alsop had gone back into the pack around 12th place. For the rest of the race the leading pair relentlessly increased the gap on the field until Kassa, running in flats, eased away on the final lap for a comfortable win. Frimley has been a happy hunting ground for Kassa for it was here that he claimed the Surrey Junior title in 1993. Nowadays he is not content with one big win per weekend and after the race it was off to an hotel in Guildford to prepare himself for winning the following day's Hog's Back Road Race.

Roger came back again to enter the funnel 10th and thus claim an Inter-Counties slot and veteran Steve Fowler proved yet again what tough guy he is, shoring up the team in 28th. Both Ray Foley and Gerry Adams went into the race below par and poor Gerry suffered badly as the distance covered increased. It was only the attentions of Mike Webb, delighted to be in the scoring six, that kept him going at the end.

While the senior men felt a little deflated, two of our young women were extremely pleased with their performances. Debbie Hearn's 6th place among the Under 17 Women was very encouraging. She looks every inch a runner and is sure to go further up the result sheets in the next few seasons. The Under 20 Women's race was held in conjunction with the seniors and Tania Sturton's 30th place overall gave her an unexpected bronze medal. Both these ladies were selected to represent Surrey in the Inter-County event.

Our more established female runners could not be tempted into action and it's all credit to those that ran that we closed in a team.

Tania Sturton runs to 3rd place in the Surrey County CC Champs for Under 20 Women.

Photo: Ray O'Donoghue

Report · page 30

What a race - but Surrey League now out of reach

REEBOK SURREY C.C. LEAGUE DIVISION ONE, RACE 3, SPARROWS DEN, 17 JAN 1998

Only one club in the league has the potential to come back from a position of 5th halfway through the series and 250-odd points behind the leaders – us! Doing it is another matter though. Still, travelling over to the Met. Police venue at West Wickham hopes were high. Gary was back in the squad, returning from his pre-Christmas complete layoff. Lee Hurst was down from Manchester and raring to go while Roger Alsop and Jim Estall were known to be in cracking form.

But this is a topsy-turvy season. The opening match, invariably the strongest for all clubs, had proved to be of a much lower standard than normal, whereas the first run of the new year – when you'd bet your shirt on the field being at its weakest – turns out to be an absolute cracker.

After the charge around the playing fields Herne Hill's Dave Taylor had already made his intentions plain. A few yards behind came Solly, Staines and a knot of Hounslow boys at the head of a bunch that included half a dozen claret and gold vests. As the colourful mass swerved off the playing fields and up along the narrow paths through the woods onto the hilly section, team managers, dogs, wives (some conspicuously pregnant) and other supporters scrambled through leafy shortcuts to get a view of the runners halfway round the first lap.

Taylor was clear and Jon Solly had a comfortable gap on the chasing group; Herne Hill one and two. The youthful Hounslow trio of Whitby, Haughian and Simpson were no respecters of Gary's reputation and were now in front of the Belgrave man. Lee Hurst was breathing down his neck while Estall, Alsop and Clive Gilby were also claiming good positions. Our forward placers were certainly outscoring Herne Hill even though they had two in front. Then there was a whole posse of red and black vests to complete their ten with runners to spare before our last scorer ran through.

"Ten in 48," shouted Hill's Stan Allen in your correspondent's direction, "What about you?"

"We're inside 60," was the reply, followed by a barely audible "but I think we've got it!"

The second half of the race unfolded and now began our troubles. A couple of our guys were starting to look uncomfortable but Steve Fowler was losing positions by the score. A suspect ankle had really gone nasty on him and as he dropped back in great pain, now our tenth man, he was was just praying that another Belgravian would come past and allow him to pull out. It didn't happen until it was too late and by that time they were approaching the finish.

At the funnel Taylor had eased off to allow Solly to get within 3 seconds of him. Then came the three Hounslow lads with Lee Hurst almost chasing down Haughian at the end. Gary Staines was next home – what a champ. turning out when far from fit – then two Aldershot men followed by the first veteran Jim Estall and Roger Alsop – great running from these two. It was 6, 7, 10, 12, 19, and when 'Gerry' and Phil Carstairs came in (good run from Phil) we went into the lead.

We knew it was going to be close – and nowhere near the clawback of points that we needed – and as it happened Herne Hill, with their men still all in 48, took another 26 points out of us on the day but both of us were surprised by the Aldershot team who packed so solidly in the 20s and early 30s.

That's it. We moved up to 4th in the league but it looks as if Hare & Hounds, with that huge advantage gained in race 1, will be the overall winners with only Aldershot in with a chance of catching them.

Things are picking up slowly in the Youths and Boys sections thanks to hard work from Reg. Hopkins. With Manuel Garcia and Robert Metalle both claiming forward positions and the ever-present Christian Morris 21st, we only wanted one more lad to begin to make an impact in the under 17 race.

Report · page 31

SOUTHERN C.C. CHAMPS. PARLIAMENT HILL FIELDS 31 JAN 1998

A disappointing day this one. For some years now we have failed to field a full team – in fact two seasons ago we had no-one on the start line at all. Somehow or other our rival South London Clubs seem to put ten or even twenty men in the start pens. It doesn't really matter if we finish well down as a team – it would just be nice to complete one. How can we begin to call ourselves a cross-country club when we don't have a cross country team to turn out for the standard events. Other clubs seem to have men's and women's teams out supporting each other in this feast of harrier racing while past runners stride the course with muddy boots and ruddy faces to cheer their teams on. For Belgrave it is a non-event. No Belgrave women were entered in any age-group, no young male Belgravians (although we note that an enterprising Christian Morris gate-crashed his way into the Youths' race without a number), and although £40 was spent on entry fees for the senior men, only four turned out.

Anyway, let's stop whinging and savour a fine run by Lee Hurst. Around 20th after the first loop, Lee worked his way through some of the top runners from the South of England. Thirteenth after two laps, he had a group of five in his sights as he went into the last circuit, a group that included SLH's Stuart Major, having a superb run, and Adrian Marriott of Hercules Wimbledon who can always be relied upon for an all-out effort. It was a hard slog and in spite of his efforts only one more man succumbed to his attack.

Very good running came from Phil Carstairs, competing in the vain hope that he would be our 6th scorer, while both Rob Harding and Don Anderson improved on their 1997 runs.

Junior Kevin Quinn, still in only the second of his three seasons as an under 20 (subject to possible age-group rule changes) was delighted with his 51st.

WOMEN'S SURREY C.C. LEAGUE DIVISION ONE WITLEY COMMON 24 JAN 1998

A good day, fast course and an eight-woman team toeing the start-line of the senior race – but the other clubs also had much stronger teams than have been the norm and the net result was that we placed 10th on the day. With one race to go in the series it looks like Division Two for the Belles next year. Nevertheless it was good to get so many runners out; there's nothing worse for a team manager than not having enough runners to make up the squad. Success will come again as long as we keep the numbers up and enjoy the sport.

Star of the day for us was Debbie Hearn who continued her progress with a very promising 4th in the under 17 women's race while Tania Sturton, a junior, led our seniors home. Below we show as much of the result as we have been able to piece together.

Report · page 34

Best South of Thames win since 1895

SOUTH OF THAMES C.C. CHAMPIONSHIP RICHMOND PARK, 14 FEB 1998

15 teams closed in

In conditions more reminiscent of early summer rather than those expected just two weeks into February, the 99th running of the South of Thames Championship saw Belgrave totally dominate the proceedings. As race time drew near the Belgrave runners just kept on coming and by the time SoT President Bill Bird brought down his handkerchief no fewer than sixteen claret and gold vests packed the starting pens.

As usual Clive Gilby showed ahead at the very beginning (does this man know any other way of running) but as the first of the two 3¾ mile laps unwound a bunch of four began to detach themselves: Gary Staines, Jim Estall, Rathbone of Brighton & Hove and Kassa Tadesse loping along in predatory fashion just behind. Clive was holding up well as our fourth scorer, Ray Foley was setting himself up for a high position and … what was this … eighteen year-old Kevin Quinn, fresh from a fine run in the Surrey Schools was our 6th man. It was going to take an absolute disaster to stop us taking the team race and on the face of it Kevin was about to get himself barred from the Junior event next Autumn when we were thinking of using him as the cornerstone of the team! Belgrave vests continued to stream past as the field circled the plantation near Pembroke Lodge to complete lap one. The twelve man trophy must surely be ours also – and dreams of a clean sweep of the individual medals also began to formulate.

Only recently returned from several weeks of training in Kenya, Kassa was indeed the man in form and over the second circuit he put half a minute between himself and Gary Staines. Now he went away to consider his options regarding the 'National' in four weeks time.

Gary's fitness is returning nicely and it always gives the team such a boost when he turns out for us; but it was no easy race for him. Maybe it was the warm conditions bringing on a touch of his asthma but Jim Estall did him no favours at all. We can admit now that Jim had to be "leaned on" to turn out for this race. He wasn't really keen on doing it and had been talking of a 90% effort. All that was thrown to the winds as soon as the race got under way and, already in a medal position, he began to attack Gary mercilessly. It says a lot for Gary's character that suffering as he was, there was no way that he was going to let another Belgravian past him – and a veteran at that! "If it had been anyone else," he later admitted, "I'd have let them go."

Jim's effort was indeed the run of the day and he was delighted.

Muttering that he'd not known it was going to be 7½ miles Clive Gilby held on grimly to his 5th spot while Ray Foley "the longer and tougher it is the better" and Mike Webb closed in the six. Mike seems to be making a habit of completing our medal winning teams.

Young Kev faded a little as the race wore on but kept ahead of a cruising Matt Yates and Graham Adams. As he finished just out of our winning six he missed the ban and can tackle that "Junior" race after all. Did he feel the distance or was he being clever?

Chas. Dickinson, Junior Galley and Mike Kazimierski completed our 12 man team and here we must pay tribute to the men who didn't score but still turned out. Having these lads on hand was what ensured that the Coleman Cup was going to be ours come what may. The total score of all sixteen of our men would still have seen us winners.

One has to go back to 1895 to find such a commanding performance. In that year on Norbury Common, with Queen Victoria still having six years of her reign ahead of her, Portsmouth Harriers took the first three medals and won the team race with 48 points. Those ancient Pompey Harriers might have pipped us by three points but did they have sixteen men out and twelve in 49?

Nearer to our own time, we have to count back 34 years to find Belgrave's previous win (Alan Black was in the twelve in 1964) and a further nine, to 1955, for the one before that (with Gordon Biscoe among the scorers). Our parenthesised Belgravians were both on hand to witness the events of 1998.
